etc. as constants, independent of the size of the set system.

Recall in
Section

3
and
Section

4
we critically relied on our hypergraph system
(V,S,G)
having intersection graphs
G_{s}
that are
\frac{1}{\polylog(N)}
-samplers. Unfortunately, we do not know how to construct such systems where
(V,S)
is additionally bounded degree in the sense that every vertex lies in at most
O(1)
hyperedges. Indeed note that
\frac{1}{\polylog(N)}
-sampling requires the sets in
S
to be size at least
\polylog(N)
, so such systems, if they indeed exist, must be extremely unbalanced with
|S|\ll|V|
. While we do not know how to rule out such systems formally, unbalanced objects of this type are notoriously difficult to construct.

Instead, we solve this issue by drawing inspiration from the global list decoding algorithm of
[
60
]
: in particular, we will add a
third layer

U
to our system consisting of subsets of
V
such that the ‘local view’ of any
u\in U
(more accurately, the hypergraph
(u,\left\{{s\cap u}\right\}_{s\in S})
) is itself a good direct product code (we’ll call these the ‘inner codes’ of
(V,S,U,G)
). Roughly speaking, we will need three key properties of
(V,S,U,G)
:

1.

The graph
(V,S)
has
constant
(left-to-right) degree (and therefore the DP code
C_{(V,S)}
is constant rate).

2.

Given any input word
w
to
(V,S)
and
f\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta_{in}}(w)
, the function
f
is in the balls of all but a
sub-constant
fraction of the inner codes
u\in U
.

3.

The hypergraph system
(V,U,G)
, which we call the ‘outer code’, has a propagation decoder for the sub-constant noise list recovery regime (in particular, we will take
(V,U)
to be
\polylog(N)
-degree to avoid the original issue).

Suppose we have a system that satisfies these requirements. Then every ‘inner code’ positioned at
u\in U
can decode a list of local functions
\mathcal{I}_{u}
labeling the vertices in
u
. By the second property, we are guaranteed all but a
\frac{1}{\polylog(N)}
-fraction of these decoded lists contain a function
\delta^{\prime}
-close to
f
, or in other words, that
f\in{\cal B}_{\frac{1}{\poly\log(N)},\delta^{\prime}}(\mathcal{I})
. But this is exactly the low-noise list recovery setting on
(V,U)
, so we may run our decoder on
(V,U)
to locally decode
f
as desired, where we query these lists
\mathcal{I}_{u}
by calling of the inner decoder.

The construction of such a set system requires is non-trivial high dimensional expander machinery. (We note that these do not follow from
[
60
]
’s ‘double samplers’ which cannot simultaneously achieve the first two properties).

Even putting aside the difficulty of constructing such systems, there’s a key issue we’ve brushed under the rug: every vertex in our local codes are only encoded in a constant number of sets (a property we critically rely on to achieve global constant rate), so our decoding accuracy
\delta^{\prime}
must also be constant (indeed this is inherent, as one can force a constant fraction of vertices in these codes to only see noise). This is of course a big problem for our original list-recovery scheme, which only achieves final accuracy
\delta_{out}\geq t\delta^{\prime}
for
t=\polylog(N)
.

To handle this, we present in this section a new list-recovery method for hypergraph systems that results in output accuracy
\delta_{out}=\delta_{in}\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
, allowing us to instantiate outer decoding in the constant rate regime. The technique relies on two key new ideas. The first, an adaptation of the ‘list pruning’ method in
[
60
]
, is to ensure the lists input to our list-recovery algorithm are
well-separated
. Roughly speaking, separation allows us to
uniquely
match lists as we walk along the decoding graph, avoiding propagation of error (as long as we avoid a tiny set of bad edge coming from sampling error). This, of course, is slightly wishful thinking; we will not be able to reduce to the case where all lists are well-separated. However, critically we
can
ensure (paying
\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
cost) that a
large constant fraction
(say
99\%
) of sets in our system have this sort of lossless propagation, and moreover that this set is identifiable to the algorithm.

Now, naively, we still have a problem: since our routing scheme takes paths of length
\polylog(N)
(and in fact we require these paths cover the graph well), it is extremely likely a random path will escape this ‘good’ set
T
above and ruin our error. This brings us to the second key idea:
subset-internal
(‘vertex-fault tolerant’) routing. In particular, we will show it is possible to construct a variant of local routing which, given oracle access to any large constant fraction of the domain
T
, locally routes between almost all
u,u^{\prime}\in T
with low congestion while
staying entirely inside
T
. Together with lossless propagation, this is sufficient to ensure accurate decoding even when our original lists have constant error and (along with the existence of the hypergraph system itself) is the key to achieving constant rate.

Section Roadmap

We give a brief outline of the structure of the section below. We first formalize the hypergraph triple setup in
Section

5.1
, along with the new notion of subset internal routing schemes. In
Section

5.2
we formalize the properties of the hypergraph triple we will use. In
Section

5.3
, we give our reduction to low noise list-recovery using the inner code structure of our triple. In
Section

5.4
we introduce the well-separated condition, and give a list recovery algorithm assuming access to a subset internal routing scheme and well-separated input lists. Finally in
Section

5.5
we show how to reduce from generic list-recovery to the well-separated setting.

5.1
Formal setup

As we mentioned in the overview, we require a set system with three layers of sets, somewhat similar to the ‘double samplers’ in
[
60
]
albeit requiring fairly different guarantees. Let
(V,S,U,G)
be a set system where
V
are the vertices,
S\subset\binom{V}{r_{1}}
,
U\subset\binom{V}{r_{2}}
consist of sets. (Note there is no requirement on
r_{1},r_{2}
.) The graph
G=(U,E)
is a decoding graph, defined analogously to its definition in
Section

2.2
, only with respect to
U
. It is a regular graph whose vertices are
U
, and for which
u\cap u^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
whenever
u\sim u^{\prime}
.
30
30

30

As in the decoding graph in
section

2.2
, not all overlapping sets have edges. The graph itself will be instance specific to our system.
The intersection graph
G_{u}
is also defined similarly: the vertices on one side are the vertices of
u
, and on the other side we put the intersections
\left\{{u\cap u^{\prime}}:{\left\{{u,u^{\prime}}\right\}\in E}\right\}
.

Along with the above, which are all analogous to graphs we analyzed on our basic hypergraph systems, we will also need several ‘new’ local and global graphs arising from
(V,S,U,G)
. On the global side, there is only one such new graph: the
(S,U)
set-layers graph. This is a bipartite graph whose vertices are
S
on one side,
U
on the other, and whose edges are all
\left\{{s,u}\right\}
such that
s\cap u\neq\emptyset
.

We now move on to the definitions of the local set systems. Denote by
In_{u}=(V_{u},S_{u})
the internal set system with
V_{u}=u
and
S_{u}=\left\{{s\cap u}:{s\in S,s\cap u\neq\emptyset}\right\}
. Denote by
G_{In_{u}}
the
local decoding graph
of
In_{u}
. That is, the vertices in this graph are
S_{u}
and, in this case unlike the above, we will connect
all

s\sim s^{\prime}
such that
s\cap s^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
31
31

31

One could define these inner graphs more generally, but in the systems we construct connecting all overlapping sets will always give the desired inner decoder so we fix the definition this way for simplicity.

As discussed above, our construction will only take the direct product over
(V,S)
as before. The extra layer of sets
U
is used only for decoding. Given an input word
w
to
\mathcal{C}_{(V,S)}
, we denote by
w_{In_{u}}
the induced local word on
S_{u}
obtained in the natural way. That is, for every
s^{\prime}=s\cap u
in
S_{u}
we take
(w_{In_{u}})_{s^{\prime}}=w_{s}|_{s^{\prime}}
. Of course, by querying the original word at
s
, we are also able to query word
s^{\prime}
.

Similarly, we also define the set system for
In_{s}=(V_{s},U_{s})
the same way. The vertices
V_{s}=s
, and the sets are all
U_{s}=\left\{{u\cap s}:{u\in U}\right\}
. We note that this set system is not necessary for the decoding itself, but its sampling properties will be important for the analysis.

5.1.1
Subset Internal Routing

As discussed in the overview, for our outer code to work in the constant rate setting we require a strengthened notion of routing on the decoder graph of
U
that only routes through
u\in U
that have lists with the distance properties mentioned in the overview (which we’ll formalize shortly below). Here we will treat this as an arbitrary subset
T
of the graph
G
(indeed we will have no control over the subset in the argument).

More formally, we now introduce our notion of ‘subset internal’ routing, which is a low congestion routing scheme that paths between vertices
v,v^{\prime}
in a graph
G=(U,E)
such that all the middle vertices between
v
and
v^{\prime}
belong to some set
T\subseteq U
with constant size. Since
T
can be arbitrary, this task may of course be impossible as stated, e.g. a certain vertex
v
may have no neighbors in
T
so one can’t reach any
v^{\prime}\neq v
without going outside of
T
. Nevertheless, we will show such schemes are possible on certain HDX if we allow our routing scheme to fail with
o(1)
probability over choice of end points
v,v^{\prime}
and relax slightly our notion of low congestion used to avoid bad events:

Definition 5.3

(Subset Internal Routing)
.

Let
G=(U,E)
be a graph. A subset internal routing scheme
\mathcal{O}^{T}(u,u^{\prime})
gets a membership oracle to some set
T\subseteq U
of size
\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{T}\right]\geq 0.9
and outputs either
FAIL
or a path
P=(u=u_{0},u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m}=u^{\prime})
such that
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{m-1}\in T
. We say that
\mathcal{O}
is
successful
if the probability it outputs
FAIL
(over a uniform choice of
u,u^{\prime}\in U
) is
\leq\frac{1}{\log^{4}|U|}
. The length of
\mathcal{O}
is the maximal length of a path in the support of
\mathcal{O}
.
\mathcal{O}
has
low congestion
if for every subset
B\subset E
, the probability
\mathcal{O}
hits
B
is at most

\Pr_{u,u^{\prime},\mathcal{O}}[\mathcal{O}(u,u^{\prime})\neq\text{FAIL}\;\wedge\;\exists e\in\mathcal{O}(u,u^{\prime}):e\in B]\leq 4t\Pr[B]^{1/4}+\frac{1}{\log^{4}|U|}

We comment that requiring
\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{T}\right]\geq 0.9
is mainly for convenience. We will construct such schemes over bipartite graphs where
U=L\cup R
, and the actual requirement we will need is that
\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{T\cap L}\right],\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{T\cap R}\right]
are both non-negligible. Similarly the constants
4,1/4
and
\frac{1}{\log^{4}|V|}
are arbitrary and fixed for simplicity to the parameters achieved by our scheme.

5.1.2
Circuit Implementation of
(V,S,U,G)

Now that we have a three part system, we will need a corresponding circuit implementation
(V,S,U,G)
analogous to that defined just for
(V,S,G)
in
Section

2.2
.

For the outer decoder, we will need the circuit implementation of the set system
(V,U)
as defined in
Section

2.2
, that is
(C_{V\to U},C_{U\to V},C_{V\to U}^{-1},C_{U\to V}^{-1},C_{U\cap U\to V})
.

On top of this, we will also need circuit implementations for
In_{u}
for every
u\in U
. We will still just work with the global names of
v\in u
or
s\in S_{u}
, rather than their labeling as
i
-th neighbors in
u
. We will need the following circuits.

1.

A circuit
C_{U\to S}(u,i)
that outputs
u
’s
i
-th neighbor in
S
(i.e. the
i
-th set
s\in S_{u}
).

2.

A circuit
C_{U;S\to V}(u,s,i)
that takes in
u\in U
,
s\in S
that intersects
u
, and index
i
. It outputs the
i
-th neighbor of
s^{\prime}=s\cap u
.

3.

A circuit
C_{U;V\to S}(u,v,i)
that takes in
u\in U
,
v\in u
, and index
i
. It outputs the
i
-th neighbor of
v

in
S_{u}
.

4.

A circuit
C_{U;S\cap S\to V}(u,s,s^{\prime},i)
that outputs the
i
-th neighbor of
u\cap s\cap s^{\prime}
or
FAIL
if
u\cap s\cap s^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

5.

Circuits
C^{-1}_{U;S\to V},C^{-1}_{U;V\to S}
that output
i
such that
C_{U;S\to V}(u,s,i)=v
or
C_{U;V\to S}(u,v,i)=s
respectively.

6.

A circuit
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
that implements the subset internal routing scheme for the decoding graph
G
.

5.2
The Set System

In the below proposition we identify the properties we use from the set system we need. This proposition is proven in
Section

6
. The parameters are set so that we can plug the system directly into our decoding framework below.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
N>0
be a large enough integer and let
\varepsilon>\frac{1}{\log\log N}
.
32
32

32

In fact, no constraints on
\varepsilon
is necessary, but outside this range of parameters the set system becomes much larger than
N
.
Set
\delta_{in}=\exp(-3\cdot 10^{3}\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
,
\eta=(\frac{\varepsilon\delta_{in}}{8})^{7}
and
\beta=(\frac{\varepsilon\delta_{in}^{2}}{\log N})^{28}
. There is a set system
(V,S,U,G)
with the following properties.

1.

|V|\in[N,N\log(N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}]
.

2.

Every
s\in S
and
u\in U
have size
\leq\log(N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
.

3.

Every vertex appears in
\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
sets.

4.

The containment graphs
(V,S),(V,U)
, the set-layers graph
(S,U)
and the local graphs
(V_{s},U_{s})
,
(V_{u},S_{u})
and the intersection graph
G_{u}
for every
s\in S
and
u\in U
are all bi-regular graphs.

5.

The intersection graph
G_{u}
is a
\beta
-sampler.

6.

The graph
(S,U)
is a
\beta
-sampler.

7.

For every
u\in U
,
(V_{u},S_{u})
is an
\eta
-sampler.

8.

For every
u\in U
, local decoding graph graph
G_{In_{u}}
is
(1-O(\eta))|S_{u}|
regular.

9.

For every
u\in U
and
s^{\prime}\in S_{u}
, the local intersection graph
(G_{In_{u}})_{s^{\prime}}
is an
\eta
-sampler.

10.

For every
s\in S
the graph
(V_{s},U_{s})
is a
\beta
-sampler.

11.

There is a low congestion subset internal routing scheme on the decoding graph of
G
of length
O(\log^{3}N\poly(\eta^{-1}))
.

We will also implement the circuits in
Section

6.7
for this set system and obtain the following guarantees.

Claim 5.5

(Circuit Implementation (Informal))
.

Let
(V,S,U,G)
be the construction in
Proposition

5.4
. Then the system is implementable in logspace uniform size
(\log N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
and depth
\tilde{O}(\log\log N\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})))
. Moreover, the set internal routing circuit
{C}_{\mathcal{O}^{T}}
makes
\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))\poly\log N
queries to the oracle in parallel.

As in prior sections, the above is informal only in the sense that we implement these circuits with some vanishing small error that does not effect the analysis (corresponding to encoding non-invertible matrices).

5.3
Reduction to List-Recovery (Inner Decoding)

In this subsection we present our alternate reduction to low noise list-recovery using the assumed sampling properties of
(V,S,U,G)
and ‘inner codes’
In_{u}=(V_{u},S_{u})
. In particular, our main goal is to show that for any input word
w
to
(V,S)
and global function
f\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta}(w)
, we will be able to approximately decode the restriction of
f
on
u
as part of a small list
\mathcal{I}_{u}
in almost every inner code
In_{u}
(roughly reducing the problem of list-recovery on
(V,U)
). Formally we show:

Lemma 5.6
.

Fix
\delta_{in},\varepsilon,\eta,\beta>0
and let
(V,S,U,G)
be as in
Proposition

5.4
. Let
w
be input word to
\mathcal{C}_{(V,S)}
and
f\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta_{in}}(w)
. Then there exists a randomized circuit
C_{list-u}
that:

1.

Takes as input
u\in U
, an index
i=1,2,\dots,\frac{10}{\varepsilon}
and
v\in V_{u}
and outputs a symbol in
\Sigma_{1}
.

2.

For
1-\beta
fraction of the random strings, there exists
1-\frac{10\beta}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}^{2}}
-fraction of the
u\in U
there exists
i
such that
C_{list-u}(u,i,\cdot)
is
O(\sqrt[3]{\delta_{in}})
-close to
f
on
u
.

3.

C_{list-u}
makes
\poly(\frac{1}{\delta_{in}},\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log\frac{1}{\beta})
queries to
w

4.

C_{list-u}
has size
(\log N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
and depth
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}(\log N)\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})))
.

To be explicit, the list
\mathcal{I}_{u}
consists of the
\frac{10}{\varepsilon}
functions
\left\{{C_{list-u}(u,i,\cdot)}\right\}_{i\in[\frac{10}{\varepsilon}]}
.

We remark the fine-grained query complexity of the above (number of bits queried from the lists rather than full symbols can be made
\tilde{O}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}^{2}})
, see
Remark

D.1
.

The proof of
Lemma

5.6
is split into three parts. The first, unsurprisingly, is to show every
In_{u}=(V_{u},S_{u})
in our hypergraph triple is a good aLLDC.

Claim 5.7
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta_{in}>0
and let
\eta
be as in
Proposition

5.4
. Let
u\in U
and
In_{u}
be a set system as in the
Proposition

5.4
, i.e.

1.

the set system
(V_{u},S_{u})
is an
\eta
-sampler.

2.

For every
s^{\prime}\in S_{u}
, the local intersection graph
33
33

33

Recall this is the graph where on one side we have the vertices in
s
, and on the other side we have intersections
s^{\prime}\cap s
for
s^{\prime}\in S_{u}
that intersect
s
.

(G_{In_{u}})_{s^{\prime}}
is an
\eta
-sampler.

3.

The local decoding graph
G_{In_{u}}
is
(1-O(\eta))|S_{u}|
regular.

Then the direct product code on
In_{u}
is a
(\delta_{in},13\sqrt[3]{\delta_{in}})
-approximate,
(\varepsilon,\frac{10}{\varepsilon},q=\poly(\frac{1}{\delta_{in}},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
-LLDC with decoding in logspace uniform size
(\log N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
size and depth
\tilde{O}(\log\log N\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})))
.

The proof of
5.7
is technical but quite similar in spirit to the original aLLDC analysis in
[
36
]
relying on sampling and density of the inner codes (albeit in a slightly different way) so is deferred to
Appendix

D
. Nevertheless, we present the algorithm here in
Figure

4
in part so we may reference it later for clarity.

Inner Decoder
InDec_{\delta_{in}}(s,v)
:

*

Input:

•

A set
s\in S_{u}
.

•

A target vertex
v\in V
.

•

Oracle access to word
w

*

Output:
a value in
\Sigma
.

1.

For
j=1,2,\dots,\frac{4\log\delta_{in}}{\varepsilon}
:

(a)

Sample a set
s^{\prime}_{j}\ni v
.

(b)

Test if:

i.

The sets
s
and
s^{\prime}_{j}
intersect, and

ii.

If
\dist_{s\cap s^{\prime}_{j}}(w_{s},w_{s^{\prime}_{j}})<4\delta_{in}
.

(c)

If
s^{\prime}_{j}
passed the test, exit the loop and output
w_{s^{\prime}_{j}}(v)

2.

If none of the
s^{\prime}
passed, output an arbitrary symbol.

Figure 4
:
Inner Decoder.
5.7
Follows by outputting
InDec_{\delta_{in}}(s,\cdot)
for
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
random choices of
s\in S_{u}
. See
Appendix

D
for formal proof.

Now, note that in our definition of list decoding, the circuits we output only need to succeed in decoding a particular function
f
with probability
\frac{3}{4}
. Therefore, even assuming
5.7
as written, we are only guaranteed to succeed in
\delta_{out}
-computing a particular
f
with constant probability.
This is obviously not sufficient to prove
Lemma

5.6
, which requires decoding
f
with high probability. We can amplify the success probability by simply repeating the decoder
O(\log\frac{1}{\beta})
times, but this blows up the list size. We will see in the next section this is a real problem, as we will pay an exponential cost in the list size to reduce to the ‘well-separated’ setting discussed in the overview, so we really need this to be constant.

Thankfully there is a simple fix to this problem (which is the second component of
Lemma

5.6
). We may run a basic pruning procedure to cut the list size down to
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
:

Claim 5.8

(List Pruning (
D.2
))
.

Fix
\tau>0
with parameters
\varepsilon,\delta_{out}
, an input word
w_{In_{u}}
, and
\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{L}\}
a set of output circuits from running the generating algorithm of the decoder
InDec_{\delta_{in}}
possibly several times. Assume that the graph
(V_{u},S_{u})
is a
\frac{\delta_{out}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}{256}
-sampler. There is a circuit
C_{Prune}
which, given oracle access to the
\{C_{i}\}
and input list, outputs a subset
L^{\prime}\subset[L]
of size
|L^{\prime}|\leq O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
. With probability at least
1-\tau
any original
C_{i}\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta_{out}}(\mathcal{L})
is
6\delta_{out}
-close to some
C_{j}\in L^{\prime}
.

Moreover
C_{Prune}
is in logspace uniform size
\poly(|L|,\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\delta_{out}},\log\frac{1}{\tau})
and depth
O(\log^{2}|L|\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta_{out}\varepsilon\tau})
, makes at most
O(\frac{\log\frac{L}{\tau}}{\max\{\varepsilon,\delta\}})
oracle calls to the
\{C_{i}\}
, word
w
, a circuit producing random
s\in S_{u}
and random
v\in V_{u}
, and the hypergraph adjacency circuits.

Again the proof is not particularly enlightening (one simply queries the input list to discard any circuits that aren’t actually computed by
w
, then finds a small cover of the rest which is promised by a bound on the combinatorial list size on the code). The proof is deferred to
Appendix

D
as well.

Now, running
5.7
several times to amplify success then pruning the resulting lists, we can clearly build a circuit like in
Lemma

5.6
that computes
f
with high probability whenever
f
is global in
In_{u}
. Thus the final step is to argue by sampling that any
f
which is global in
(V,S)
should also be global in almost every local code
In_{u}
by sampling.

Claim 5.9
.

Fix any
\varepsilon,\delta,\beta>0
with
\delta\leq 4\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, and let
(V,S,U,G)
be such that

1.

(S,U)
is a
\beta
-sampler.

2.

For every
s\in S
the graph
(V_{s},U_{s})
is a
\beta
-sampler.

Then for any
f\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta}(w)
:

\underset{u\in U}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{f|_{u}\in\mathcal{B}_{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},2\delta}(w_{In_{u}})}\right]\geq 1-\frac{9\beta}{\delta^{2}_{in}\varepsilon}

Here one should think of
\beta
as
\frac{1}{\poly\log(N)}
and
\delta
as some constant multiple of
\delta_{in}
from above. We remark the condition on
\delta
is just for calculational simplicity and has no effect in later arguments as we will be taking
\delta\leq\exp(-1/\varepsilon)
anyway. We also note that if it had been the case that
f\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,0}(w)
,
5.9
would follow immediately from sampling of
(S,U)
. However, because
f\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta}(w)
, a priori it could be the case that
\dist_{s}(g,f)<\delta
but that the distance increases upon intersecting with
u
. In this case
g|_{s\cap u}
would not sufficiently compute
f|_{u}
and we may fail to decode
f
. Thankfully, sampling of
(V_{s},U_{s})
ensures this event is rare.

Proof of
5.9
.

Fix
f
and let
A\subseteq S
be the sets where
w_{s}
is
\delta
-close to
f
. By definition
\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]\geq\varepsilon
. If
f
is not global in
In_{u}
then one of the following events occurred:

1.

Either
\underset{s\sim u}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]<\frac{3\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]}{4}
, or

2.

More than
\frac{\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]}{4}
sets
s\in A
neighboring
u
have
w_{s}

\delta
-close to
f
but

\dist_{s\cap u}(f,w|_{s\cap u})>2\delta.

This is because if neither the first nor second items are satisfied, there is at least
\frac{\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]}{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
fraction of
s\sim u
such that
\dist_{s\cap u}(f,w|_{s\cap u})\leq 2\delta
as desired.

Thus it is enough to bound the probability of the two events. Note the first is bounded by
\frac{16\beta}{\varepsilon^{2}}\leq\frac{\beta}{\delta^{2}\varepsilon}
by the global sampling property between
(S,U)
.

Toward bounding the second, for any
s\in A
call a pair
(s,u)
with
s\sim u

misleading
if
\dist(f,w_{s})<\delta
but
\dist_{s\cap u}(f,w_{s}|_{s\cap u})>2\delta
. By
\beta
-sampling of
(V_{s},U_{s})
, for every
s\in A
there are at most
\frac{\beta}{\delta^{2}}
neighbors
u\sim s
such that
(s,u)
is misleading, so there are at most
\frac{\beta}{\delta^{2}}
misleading pairs overall. By Markov’s inequality,

\underset{u\in U}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\underset{s\sim u}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{(u,s)\text{ is misleading}}\right]>\frac{\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]}{4}}\right]\leq\frac{4\underset{u,s}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{(u,s)\text{ is misleading}}\right]}{\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]}.

The numerator is upper bounded by
\frac{4\beta}{\delta^{2}}
by the above, and the denominator is bounded by
\varepsilon
.

On the other hand, if
u
satisfies the first item but violates the second item in the list above, then it contributes to
\underset{u\in U}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\underset{s\sim u}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{(u,s)\text{ is misleading}}\right]>\frac{\underset{}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{A}\right]}{4}}\right]
. Hence there are at most
\frac{8\beta}{\delta^{2}\varepsilon}
such
u
. The claim holds by adding up this probability and the probability of violating the first item.
∎

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

5.6
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.6
.

Fix any input word
w
to
(V,S)
, and
f\in\mathcal{B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta_{in}}(w)
. The circuit
C_{list-u}
, on input
(u,i,v)
, runs the
In_{u}
-inner decoder
O(\log\frac{1}{\beta})
times
34
34

34

Note we must be careful about how exactly
C_{list-u}
accesses the described inner decoder here. Namely we do not have to hardcode a separate inner code for every
u
into the circuit (which would massively blow up its size). Instead, as described shortly below, we can simply use
L
copies of the
InDec(s,g_{i},\cdot)
circuit in
Figure

4
combined with our circuit implementation to ensure the
s
fed in (and queried in the procedure) are the appropriate neighbors of
u
.
and feeds the resulting
L=O(\frac{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{\varepsilon})
circuits
C_{1},\ldots,C_{L}
into
C_{prune}
with failure probability
\tau\leq O(\beta^{2})
. The circuit
C_{prune}
outputs the indexes of
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
of circuits surviving the prune. Finally,
C_{list-u}
uses
i
to index into the output subset
L^{\prime}\subset[L]
of
C_{prune}
and produces
C_{L^{\prime}(i)}(v)
.
35
35

35

If it happens to be the case that a
L^{\prime}
is smaller than the number of indices, the output may be arbitrary on larger
i
.

We need to argue that across the internal randomness of
C_{list-u}
, a
1-\beta
fraction of the time there exists a
1-\frac{10\beta}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}^{2}}
fraction of
u\in U
for which
C_{list-u}(u,i,\cdot)

O(\sqrt[3]{\delta_{in}})
-computes
f
. To see this, note that by
5.9
we are guaranteed that
f\in\mathcal{B}_{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},2\delta_{in}}(w_{In_{u}})
for all except a
\frac{9\beta}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}^{2}}
fraction of
u\in U
. Since we have run the inner decoder
O(\log\frac{1}{\beta})
times, by
5.8
for every such ‘good’
u
we are assured that with probability at least
1-O(\beta^{2})
over the internal randomness we indeed output a deterministic circuit
O(\sqrt[3]{\delta_{in}})
-computing
f
. Thus by Markov’s inequality, the probability over the internal randomness that more than a
\beta
fraction of these good
u\in U
fail to have such an index is at most
\beta
, leaving at most a
\frac{10\beta}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}^{2}}
fraction with no good index computing
f
as desired.

Circuit complexity

By
5.7
, the inner decoder circuit is implementable in size
(\log N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
and depth
\tilde{O}(\log(\log N)\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})))
. The list pruning procedure makes
\poly(\log N)\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
parallel calls to the oracles of
\left\{{C_{i}}\right\}
. Finally, the output of the inner decoder is fed to our list pruning circuit instantiated with confidence
\tau=(\log^{6}N\cdot\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})))^{-1}
,
L=\poly(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\log\log N)
, and
\delta_{out}=O(\delta_{in}^{2/3})=\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
, which does not increase the asymptotic size but does increase the depth to
\tilde{O}(\log^{2}(\log N)\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

∎

5.4
List Recovery from Constant Accuracy (Outer Decoding)

The prior section gave us a new method of reducing to the low noise list-recovery regime, namely for constructing lists
\mathcal{I}_{u}
for every
u\in U
such that any fixed
(\delta,\varepsilon)
-global function
f
in
(V,S)
will appear in almost all lists. Thus, as in
Section

3
, we now just need a list recovery algorithm on
(V,U)
that recovers functions that are
(\delta,1-\frac{1}{\poly\log(N)})
-global in the lists
\mathcal{I}=\left\{{\mathcal{I}_{u}}\right\}
.

As before, at a high level our list recovery algorithm for
(V,U)
will start from a random outer set
u
and a function
g\in\mathcal{I}_{u}
that is
\delta_{in}
-close to the global function
f
. To decode
v
, we route to a random
u^{\prime}\ni v
we’d like to decode and hope to identify through this process some
g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{u^{\prime}}
that is close to
f
and output
g^{\prime}(v)
. In
Theorem

3.4
, we showed by tracking the list of functions close to the initial
g
, we can ensure the final
g^{\prime}
output is

O(\delta_{in}\polylog(N))
-close to
f
.

Unfortunately, as we’ve discussed above, this is insufficient for the constant rate setting where
\delta_{in}
(the decoding accuracy of our inner codes) is also constant.
36
36

36

This is inherent in any direct product code with constant rate (or even other codes with constant rate and locality). The rate of a direct product code is the average degree of a vertex in
(V,S)
, which we denote by
d
. We can always find a vertex set
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
of relative size
\frac{1}{2d}
whose neighborhood in
S
has size
\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Denote such a set
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
. Now if we take a codeword
Enc(f)
and erase/replace-by-noise the sets in
Enc(f)
in the neighborhood of
V^{\prime}
, we still get a word that is
\frac{1}{2}
-close to
Enc(f)
. However, in such a word all information on
V^{\prime}
is lost, so we cannot hope to recover any of the bits of
V^{\prime}
(even in a non-local algorithm). In particular, we are limited to decoding functions that are but
\frac{1}{2d}
-close even when
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2}
.
This means we cannot afford anywhere near a
\polylog(N)
multiplicative blowup in error, and we need a new approach to propagation decoding. In this section we present our new method based on our strengthened notion of subset-internal routing that gives a bound of
\delta_{out}=\delta_{in}\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
N>0
,
\delta_{0},\beta>0
such that
\beta<\left(\frac{\varepsilon\delta_{0}}{\log N}\right)^{28}
. Let
(V,U)
be a set system satisfying:

1.

V
has
\leq N^{2}
vertices.
37
37

37

Technically we would like
V
to have exactly
N
vertices.
Proposition

5.4
only promises approximately that, but the bound of
N^{2}
is more than enough.

2.

U
has a subset internal routing scheme of maximal length
O(\log^{3}N\exp(\frac{5}{\varepsilon}))
.

3.

For every
u\in U
, the intersection graph
G_{u}
is a
\beta
-sampler.

Then
\mathcal{C}_{(V,U)}
is a
(\delta_{0},8^{103/\varepsilon}\delta_{0}))
-approximate,
\frac{10}{\varepsilon}
-input
(1-\frac{1}{\log^{10}N},\tilde{O}(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}),\frac{\poly\log N}{\delta_{0}}\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon})))
-LLRC.

Note the somewhat overcomplicated parameters are fixed so we can plug in our inner code directly to this result.

The first key idea to achieve list-recovery from constant
\delta_{in}
is an adaptation of the ‘list pruning’ method in
[
60
]
which ensures the lists input to our list-recovery algorithm are
well-separated
.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{u}
be a list of functions on some domain
u
.

•

We say that a list
L\subset\mathcal{I}_{u}
is
\delta
-dense (with respect to
\mathcal{I}_{u}
) if every function
g\in\mathcal{I}_{u}
is
\delta
-close to some function
g^{\prime}\in L
.

•

We say that a list
L\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{u}
is
\delta^{\prime}
-separated if for any pair of distinct functions
g_{1},g_{2}\in L
,
\dist(g_{1},g_{2})>\delta^{\prime}
.

•

We say that a list
L\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{u}
is
\delta
-
well separated
if it is
\delta
-dense and
6\delta
-separated.

We remark the constant
6
is arbitrary (any constant larger than
2
would suffice, the above is fixed to simplify calculations).

Roughly speaking, if every list was well separated
at the same scale
\delta
, it would allow us to
uniquely
match lists as we walk along the decoding graph, avoiding additional errors in each step. Unfortunately, while it is possible to ensure every list is
\delta
-well-separated for some
\delta
, we do not know how to ensure they are separated at the same scale which is critical for this sort of lossless propagation. Instead, we will show how to ensure below that a
large constant fraction
of sets in our system, say
99\%
, are well-separated at the same scale and therefore have lossless propagation (and moreover this can be done in such a way that the condition is testable). Denoting by
T\subseteq U
be the set of
u\in U
where we can get such well separated lists, the problem then reduces to subset-internal routing inside
T
which we have by assumption (and will construct explicitly in
Section

6.4
).

We are now ready to present the decoding circuit of the outer decoder in
Figure

5
. It is based on the following components which we abstract:

1.

An implementation of the lists
\mathcal{I}=\left\{{\mathcal{I}_{u}}\right\}_{u\in U}
:
Recall in reality we start from an input word on sets
s\in S
, not for
u\in U
. The circuit
C_{list-u}
from
Lemma

3.5
, allows us to inner-decode
w
to lists
\mathcal{I}_{u}
and simulate queries to functions in them. We stress again that the important thing about
\left\{{\mathcal{I}_{u}}\right\}_{u\in U}
, is that functions
f
which are
(\varepsilon,\delta_{in})
-computed by the original word
w
on
S
have a close-by function in a
1-\frac{1}{\poly\log(N)}
-fraction of the constructed lists on
U
. We state the theorem on the outer decoder in terms of the lists
\mathcal{I}
. We get back to the actual circuit implementation when we calculate circuit complexity.

2.

A list well-separation circuit
: As introduced above, we need a circuit
C_{T}
that takes as input a list
\mathcal{I}_{u}
and some
j
, and either outputs
FAIL
or a list
L_{u}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{u}
that is
1.168^{j}\delta_{in}
-well-separated inside
\mathcal{I}_{u}
. We prove the next lemma in the following subsection.

Lemma 5.12
.

For every
\delta_{0}>0
there is a randomized circuit
C^{\delta_{0}}_{T}
that takes as input
u
(and some description of the list
\mathcal{I}_{u}
) of size
\frac{10}{\varepsilon}
, and an index
j\in\left\{{1,2,\dots,\frac{1000}{\varepsilon}}\right\}
. It either outputs
FAIL
or a list
L_{u}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{u}
with the following guarantees.

(a)

For any
\mathcal{I}_{u}
, for at most a
0.01
-fraction of the
j\in\left\{{1,2,\dots,\frac{1000}{\varepsilon}}\right\}
, it outputs
FAIL
with probability
>\frac{1}{\poly\log N}
.

(b)

If
C^{\delta_{0}}_{T}
did not output
FAIL
, then with probability
1-\frac{1}{\poly\log N}
, the output list
L_{u}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{u}
is
\delta_{j}^{\prime}
-well separated in
\mathcal{I}_{u}
, for
\delta_{j}^{\prime}=1.16\cdot 8^{j}\delta_{0}
.

The
\poly\log N
in the guarantees can be any polynomial we choose, and in particular set to
\frac{1}{\log^{10}N}
.

The proof of this lemma is presented in the next section, where we also analyze its circuit complexity. For now, we take it at face value and just proceed assuming it is possible to construct this circuit.

3.

A subset internal routing scheme
: denoted
\mathcal{O}^{T}
. To be explicit, this routing scheme takes as input a pair
u,u^{\prime}\in U
and is supposed to output a path from
u
to
u^{\prime}
such that all vertices (except, perhaps, the endpoints) are in the set
T
. Such a routing scheme exists by
Proposition

5.4
. Here the set
T\subseteq U
is the set of
u
such that
C^{\delta_{0}}_{T}(u,j)
outputs FAIL with probability at most
\frac{1}{\log^{10}(N)}
(following property (a) of
Lemma

5.12
), where
j
will be given to the decoder as a parameter (and in particular, will be sampled at random). Formally, when we call the
T
-membership oracle on a set
u^{\prime\prime}\in U
, we apply
C^{\delta_{0}}_{T}
on it and check whether or not it failed.

Outer decoder
OutDec_{\delta_{0},j}(u,g,v)
:

*

Input:

•

u\in U
and a function
g\in\mathcal{I}_{u}
.

•

v\in V
.

•

Parameters
j
and
\delta_{0}
.

*

Output:
A value in
\Sigma_{1}
.

1.

Let
T\subseteq U
bet the set of all
u
such that
C^{\delta_{0}}_{T}(u,j)
does not fail.

2.

Sample a random
u^{\prime}\in U
such that
u^{\prime}\ni v
.

3.

Call
\mathcal{O}^{T}(u,u^{\prime})
. If the routing scheme itself failed then abort and output an arbitrary symbol. Otherwise let
P=(u=u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{t}=u^{\prime})
be its output path, and for
k<t
, denote by
L_{u_{k}}
the list output by
C_{T}(u_{k},j)
.

4.

For
k=1,\dots,t-1
:

(a)

Find some
g_{k}\in L_{u_{k}}
such that
\dist_{u_{k}\cap u_{k-1}}(g_{k-1},g_{k})<3\cdot\delta_{j}^{\prime}
.

(b)

If there are several such functions, choose one of them uniformly at random.

5.

Find any
g_{t}\in\mathcal{I}_{u_{t}}
such that
\dist_{u_{t}\cap u_{t-1}}(g_{t-1},g_{t})<3\cdot\delta_{j}^{\prime}
and output
g_{t}(v)
.

Figure 5
:
Outer decoder circuit algorithm

Our generating algorithm samples a random
u_{0}
, a random
j\in[\frac{1000}{\varepsilon}]
, and outputs circuits
\left\{{OutDec_{\delta_{0},j_{i}}(u_{0},g,\cdot)}\right\}_{g\in\mathcal{I}_{u}}
.

We are now ready to prove the main result of the subsection, list-recovery from subset-internal routing schemes:

Proof of
Theorem

5.10
.

The proof is similar to
Theorem

3.4
. Fix lists
\mathcal{I}
and
f\in{\cal B}_{\frac{1}{\log^{6}N},\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{I})
. Our goal is to prove for most
u_{0}
and choices of
j\in[\frac{1000}{\varepsilon}]
, there exists
g\in\mathcal{I}_{u_{0}}
such that
OutDec_{\delta_{0},j}(u_{0},g,\cdot)
approximately computes
f
:

\underset{u_{0},j}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{\exists g\in\mathcal{I}_{u_{0}}:\underset{v,r}{\mathbb{P}}\left[{OutDec_{\delta_{0},j}(u_{0},g,v)=f(v)}\right]\geq 1-\delta_{1}}\right]\geq\frac{3}{4}

In particular, the generating algorithm will succeed with high probability by trying all
g\in\mathcal{I}_{u_{0}}
.

Toward this end, let
T=T_{j}
be the set of
u
such that
C^{\delta_{0}}_{T}(u,j)
outputs FAIL with probability at most
\frac{1}{\log^{10}(N)}
, and recall we require
|T|\geq 0.9|U|
for subset internal routing. By
Lemma

5.12
property (a), the expected size of
T
over a random
j
is
0.99|U|
, so the probability that
|T|<0.9|U|
is at most
0.1
by Markov.

Now, condition on having sampled a good
j
with
|T|\geq 0.9|U|
. As in
Theorem

3.4
, we now define a few further rare ‘bad events’ such that outside these circumstances our decoder succeeds. In particular, let
B
denote the set of edges
\left\{{u,u^{\prime}}\right\}
such that for either
u^{\prime\prime}\in\left\{{u,u^{\prime}}\right\}
one of the following conditions holds:

1.

Bad List:
There is no
g^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{u^{\prime\prime}}
that is
\delta_{0}
-close to
f
.

2.

Bad Sampling:
There exists some function
g^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{u^{\prime\prime}}
such that

\left|{\dist_{u^{\prime\prime}}(f,g^{\prime\prime})-\dist_{u\cap u^{\prime}}(f,g^{\prime\prime})}\right|>\delta_{0}.

We call a path
(u_{0},u_{1},\dots,u_{m})
good if no edge in the path is in
B
. We claim that it is enough to prove that most
u_{0}
have mostly good oracle paths to a random endpoint
u_{t}
:

\Pr_{u_{0},j}\left[\Pr_{u_{t},\mathcal{O}^{T}}[\mathcal{O}^{T}(u_{0},u_{t})\text{ doesn't fail and the path is good}]\geq 1-\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}\right]\geq 99\%

(5)

Note that the inner probability implicitly depends on
j
since
T
is defined with respect to
C_{T}(\cdot,j)
.

Let’s first complete the proof assuming (
5
). Fix any
u_{0},j
such that the inner condition holds. By assumption, there exists some
g\in\mathcal{I}_{u_{0}}
that’s
\delta_{0}
-close to
f
. We claim that for any such fixed choice, the probability
OutDec_{\delta_{0},j}(u_{0},g,v)=f(v)
on a random
v
is at least
1-\delta_{1}
. Union bounding over the choice of good
j
and
u_{0}
gives the final desired decoding guarantee.

To see this, first recall that on input
v
, the decoder draws a random
u_{t}\ni v
, paths to it via
\mathcal{O}^{T}(u_{0},u_{t})
and outputs
g_{t}(v)
for some valid choice of
g_{t}\in L_{u_{t}}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{t}
. Thus our goal is to lower bound

\Pr_{r,v}[OutDec_{j,\delta_{0}}(u_{0},g,v)=f(v)]=\Pr_{v\in u_{t},\mathcal{O}^{T},g_{t}}[g_{t}(v)=f(v)]

where
g_{t}\in L_{u_{t}}
is the function obtained by the last step of the algorithm, and we recall
L_{u_{t}}\subset\mathcal{I}_{u_{t}}
is the output list of the well-separation circuit
C_{T}(u_{t},j)
. We note on both sides if
\mathcal{O}^{T}
fails or there is no valid choice of
g_{t}
, we do not count this in the probability.

Now examining the right-hand probability, notice we have drawn
v\in V
uniformly at random, then a random neighbor
u_{t}\in v
. Since
(V,U)
is regular, this is the same as first drawing a random
u_{t}\in U
, then sub-sampling a random vertex
v\in u_{t}
. This means it is enough to argue that, over the randomness of
u_{t},\mathcal{O}^{T}
, the final function
g_{t}
on
u_{t}
is
\delta_{1}
-close to
f
with high probability. Let
L_{u_{t}}^{\prime}\subseteq L_{u_{t}}
be the possible functions that are
3\delta_{0}
close to
g_{t-1}
.

\Pr_{u_{t},\mathcal{O}^{T}}\left[\forall g_{t}\in L_{u_{t}}^{\prime}:\text{dist}(g_{t},f)\leq\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}\right]\geq 1-\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}

In particular, the probability we correctly output
f(v)
over a random choice of
u_{t}\in U,g_{t}\in L_{u_{t}}^{\prime}
and
v\in u_{t}
is at least
(1-\frac{\delta_{1}}{2})^{2}\leq 1-\delta_{1}
as desired.

Now to show the above, we introduce one final bad event outside which our decoder succeeds: the event that our distance tester errs (recall we sub-sample points in the intersection to test distance rather than check the full symbol to ensure our decoding circuits remain small). By construction (and
F.1
), this occurs with probability at most
\frac{\delta_{1}}{4}
.

Now condition on: the routing oracle not failing, the
\mathcal{O}^{T}(u_{0},u_{t})
being good
and
the above event that our distance tester doesn’t err on the path (which occurs with probability at least
1-\delta_{1}
by a union bound
38
38

38

Here we also use the fact that
j
chosen above makes the failing probability
\frac{1}{\log^{4}N}
.
). We claim under this conditioning
every

g_{t}\in L_{u_{t}}^{\prime}
is
\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}
-close to
f
when we start from the promised
g=g_{0}
that is
\delta_{0}
-close to
f
.

This is where the proof differs from
Theorem

3.4
. By the goodness of the path, for every
u_{k}
there is a function that is
\delta_{0}
-close to
f
in the list
\mathcal{I}_{u_{k}}
. By the
\delta_{j}^{\prime}
-well separatedness of every
L_{u_{k}}
(for
k=1,2,\dots,t-1
), there is a
unique
function
g^{\prime}\in L_{u_{k}}
that is
\delta_{j}^{\prime}+\delta_{0}
-close to
f
. We claim that this function is the function that is chosen in every step of the algorithm. This follows by induction on
k
. For
k=0
, this follows because
g=g_{0}
is
\delta_{0}
-close to
f
by assumption. Now assume it is true for
g_{k-1}
and prove for
g_{k}
. Our assumption is that
\dist_{u_{k-1}}(g_{k-1},f)<(6^{j}+1)\delta_{0}
. Therefore by goodness of the edge, on the intersection

\dist_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}}(g_{k-1}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}},f)<\delta_{j}^{\prime}+2\delta_{0}.

Now on the one hand, if
g_{k}
is the function that is
\delta_{j}^{\prime}+\delta_{0}
-close to
f
, then also in the intersection

\dist_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}}(g_{k}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}},f)<\delta_{j}^{\prime}+2\delta_{0}

and therefore by the triangle inequality,

\dist_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}}(g_{k-1}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}},g_{k}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}})<2\delta_{j}^{\prime}+2\delta_{0}\leq 3\cdot\delta_{j}^{\prime}.

On the other hand, for any other
g^{\prime}\in L_{u_{k}}
, by well separatedness, it is
5\delta_{j}^{\prime}-\delta_{0}
-far from
f
.
39
39

39

This is just using the fact that
f
is
\delta_{j}^{\prime}+\delta_{0}
-close to
g_{k}
, that
g_{k}
and
g^{\prime}
are
6\delta_{j}^{\prime}
-far apart and the reverse triangle inequality.
By goodness of the edge, in the intersection

\dist_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}}(f|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}},g^{\prime}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}})>5\delta_{j}^{\prime}-2\delta_{0}.

By the (reverse) triangle inequality,

\dist_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}}(g_{k-1}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}},g^{\prime}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}})>5\delta_{j}^{\prime}-2\delta_{0}-\dist_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}}(g_{k-1}|_{u_{k-1}\cap u_{k}},f)

and by the bound above this is at least
5\delta_{j}^{\prime}-2\delta_{0}-(\delta_{j}^{\prime}+2\delta_{0})=4\delta_{j}^{\prime}-4\delta_{0}>3\cdot\delta_{j}^{\prime}
. Therefore
g_{k}
and only
g_{k}
can be chosen in the
k
-th step.

In the final step, we do not have the guarantee that there is a single function that will pass the test, but by a similar argument to the above, the function
g_{t}
will be
3\delta_{j}^{\prime}
-close to
f
, and this is less than
\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}
.

The rest of the proof continues to follow the same lines of
Theorem

3.4
. It is left to prove
Equation

5
, that many
u_{0},j
have few bad paths. To see this, first observe that by Markov’s inequality it is enough to prove a random choice of
u_{0},u_{t}
, and path
\mathcal{O}(u_{0},u_{t})
, the probability
\mathcal{O}^{T}(u_{0},u_{t})
either fails or hits
B
is very low, say:

\Pr_{u_{0},u_{t},\mathcal{O}^{T}}[\mathcal{O}^{T}(u_{0},u_{t})\text{ fails or hits }B]\leq\frac{1}{\log^{2}N}.

This is the regime that is bounded by low-congestion routing. In particular, since
\mathcal{O}^{T}
is low congestion, it is enough to prove the measure of
B
is small, namely
\Pr[B]\leq\frac{1}{\log^{9}N}
. This follows essentially immediately from sampling, and from the promise that all but a
\frac{1}{\log^{10}N}
-fraction of
u\in U
’s list contain a function
\delta_{0}
-close to
f
. The latter ensures the probability a random edge satisfies the ‘Bad List’ condition is at most
\frac{2}{\log^{10}N}
by a union bound. The former says that for any fixed
u
, the probability a random neighbor
u^{\prime}
mis-samples any fixed function in the list of
u
is at most
\frac{\beta}{\delta_{in}^{2}}
. Union bounding over the lists of both
s
and
s^{\prime}
in a random edge, this event therefore occurs with probability at most
\frac{2\beta}{\varepsilon\delta_{0}^{2}}
, and a final union bound gives
\Pr[B]\leq\frac{1}{\log^{9}N}
as desired.
∎

5.5
Reducing to Well-Separated Lists

We now give a formal proof of
Lemma

5.12
by constructing our list sparsification circuit
C_{T}^{\delta_{0}}
.

Unfortunately, it is not true that any list generated by our inner codes is automatically well-spread (indeed it will almost certainly not be). It turns out, however, that it
is
possible to prune the lists in such a way that at many ‘scales’
j
, we can find sub-lists that are
\delta_{j}^{\prime}
-well-separated for
\delta_{j}^{\prime}\approx\delta_{in}8^{i}
. The argument is an iterated variant of a similar method in
[
60
]
who proved one such scale exists.

We present our pruning procedure toward this end in
Figure

6
, which is based on the following basic object we call a ‘distance graph’. Namely given a list of functions
L
, we prune
L
by analyzing the (empirical)
(L,\delta_{0})
-distance graphs which have vertex set
L
, and edge set all
\left\{{g,g^{\prime}}\right\}
such that
\dist(g,g^{\prime})\leq\delta_{0}
measured empirically over a random sub-samples of the domain. We note that technically the circuit
C_{T}^{\delta_{0}}
is a randomized circuit, but as explained in the proof, it’s error is
\frac{1}{\polylog N}
, and so we can safely run it without ruining the probability that the outer decoder succeeds.

List sparsifier
C_{T}^{\delta_{0}}
:

*

Input:

•

The list
\mathcal{I}_{u}
.

•

An index
j\in\left\{{1,2,\dots,\frac{1000}{\varepsilon}}\right\}
.

*

Output:
Either
FAIL
or a list
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{u}
.

1.

Sample
O(\frac{\log\frac{\log N}{\varepsilon}}{\delta_{0}})
random points from the domain and compute the empirical distance between every pair
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{u}
.

2.

Set
\delta_{k}=8^{k}\delta_{0}
and
L_{0}=\mathcal{I}_{u}
. Compute
G_{0}
, the empirical
(L_{0},\delta_{0})
-distance graph.

3.

For
k=1,2,\dots,\frac{1000}{\varepsilon}
:

(a)

Find a maximally independent set
L_{k}\subseteq L_{k-1}
in
G_{k-1}
that was computed in the prior round (or before the loop in the case of
G_{0}
).

(b)

Compute
G_{k}
, the
(L_{k},\delta_{k})
-distance graph.

4.

If the subset
L_{j}
is
\delta_{j+1}
-separated output
L_{j}
(according to the empirical distances), otherwise output
FAIL
.

Figure 6
:
Outer decoder circuit algorithm

We comment that the algorithm runs for
\frac{1000}{\varepsilon}
rounds regardless of the output only depending on the first
j
rounds. This peculiar choice is just for ease of analysis and ease of implementation in a circuit. We can just as well stop the loop when
k=j
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.12
.

The only randomized part in our algorithm is the computation of distances between every two functions in our list. Sample
O(\frac{\log\frac{\log N}{\varepsilon}}{\delta_{0}})
random points from the domain and compute the empirical distance between every pair
g,g^{\prime}\in L
. By a standard Chernoff and union bound, for every
\delta_{k}=\delta_{0}8^{k}
, the probability that any pair
g,g^{\prime}
has
d(g,g^{\prime})\leq 7.9\delta_{k}
but has empirical distance greater than
8\delta_{k}
is at most
\frac{1}{\polylog N}
, and similarly for when
d(g,g^{\prime})\geq 8.1\delta_{k}
but measures as less than
8\delta_{k}
. Condition on no such events occurring.

We observe that in every step
L_{k}
is
8.1\delta_{k-1}
-dense in
L_{k-1}
. This is because if there were a function
f\in L_{k-1}
that is farther than
8.1\delta_{k-1}
from any function in
L_{k}
, then by the conditioning above, no edge between
f
and
L_{k-1}
will appear in the distance graph
G_{k}
, which contradicts maximality of
L_{k-1}
. By using the triangle inequality, for every function in
f_{0}\in L_{0}
there is a function in
f_{1}\in L_{1}
that is
8.1\delta_{0}
-close to it. There is a function
f_{2}\in L_{2}
that is
8.1\delta_{1}
-close to
f_{1}
etc. Thus concludes via the triangle inequality that for every
f_{0}\in L_{0}
there is an
f_{k}\in L_{k}
such that

\dist(f_{0},f_{k})\leq\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\dist(f_{m},f_{m+1})\leq 8.1\delta_{0}\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}8^{m}\leq\frac{8.1}{7}8^{k-1}\delta_{0}.

In particular,
L_{j}
is
1.16\delta_{j-1}
-dense in
L_{0}
.

Next we observe that if
L_{j}
was output, then it is
7.9\delta_{j}
-separated. Indeed, if
L_{j}
was output, then the empirical distances of any two functions in
L_{j}
were
\geq 8\delta_{j}
, so by the event we conditioned on, their actual distances are at lesat
7.9\delta_{j}
. In particular, for
\delta_{j}^{\prime}=1.16\cdot 8^{j}\delta_{0}
, if
L_{j}
is output, then it is
\delta_{j}^{\prime}
-well separated.

Finally, note that if
L_{j}
is not output, then it means that
L_{j+1}\neq L_{j}
and in particular, that
|L_{j+1}|\leq|L_{j}|-1
. Note that after
\frac{10}{\varepsilon}-1
failures, the list just becomes a singleton, in which case we will always succeed. Thus there are at most a
0.1
-fraction of indexes where we fail for any given list.

∎

Circuit Complexity

Finally, we sketch the standard fact that the above algorithm can be implemented logspace uniform size and depth.

Claim 5.13
.

The circuit
C_{T}^{\delta_{0}}
is implementable in
\poly(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\delta_{0}},\log\log N)
size,
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\log^{2}\frac{\log N}{\varepsilon\delta_{0}}))
depth and makes at most
O(\frac{\log\frac{\log N}{\varepsilon}}{\delta_{0}})
calls to the list circuit
C_{list-u}
and point circuits
C_{U\to V}
.

Proof.

The number of oracle calls is immediate from the description. The first step of the algorithm calls the sample oracle in parallel. Then the algorithm runs parallel pairwise distance tests for every pair of functions in
\mathcal{I}_{u}
(at every scale that will be tested in the following iterative procedure). This corresponds to calling
O(|\mathcal{I}_{u}|)^{2}
equality circuits, and
O(|\mathcal{I}_{u}|)^{3}
Threshold circuits (since there are
O(|\mathcal{I}_{u}|)
iterations and
\binom{|\mathcal{I}_{u}|}{2}
pairs of distinct functions).

Now, for the iterative component of the algorithm, it is enough to have a circuit which takes as input the current indicator of the list
L_{k-1}\in\{0,1\}^{|\mathcal{I}_{u}|}
and outputs the indicator for
L_{k}
, along with a bit indicating whether or not the algorithm output FAIL at that round. Note that the circuit may also be assumed to have access to the distance graph for the round, since it knows the vertex set
L_{k-1}
and the distance threshold computations give the edges. This means it is enough to compute a maximal independent set on this input graph which is in
NC^{2}
by a result of Luby
[
96
]
. To check whether FAIL is output, the algorithm simply checks if the indicator of the output maximal independent set is equal to the original input.

All of the above procedures are in
NC^{2}
over the domain of sampled points. Wiring the circuits for each of the
O(|\mathcal{I}_{u}|)
scales in sequence results in the desired uniform size and depth.
∎

Remark 5.14
.

If one is fine with a blowup of
\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}))
instead of
\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
, a simpler algorithm is possible: on input
j\leq\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}))
just find a maximal independent set in the
(\mathcal{I}_{u},5^{j}\delta_{0})
-distance graph. The algorithm outputs a maximal independent set for every
j
such that no pair of functions
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{u}
have distance between
5^{j}\delta_{0}
and
5^{j+1}\delta_{0}
which is well-separated as any such set is
6^{j}\delta_{0}
-dense, but also
6^{j+1}\delta_{0}
-separated. As there are
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
distinct possible distances between pairs of functions in
L
, by allowing
O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})
possible indexes
j
, we can get large constant success rate. As we do not use this later on, we omit the details and just comment that using this procedure instead has the slight advantage of reducing the dependence on
\varepsilon
in the circuit depth.

5.6
Putting it All Together (Modulo Circuits)

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

5.1
(defering the circuit complexity analysis to the next subsection). Fix
\varepsilon>0
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. We take the set system
(V,S,U,G)
promised in
Proposition

5.4
. The parameters
\beta,\eta
in the theorem statement are chosen so that they are small enough for using all claims and lemmas in this section. The resulting code is the direct product
\mathcal{C}_{(V,S)}
. The hyperedge set sizes are
\log(N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
so locality and alphabet size follow by direct computation. Since
(V,S)
is bi-regular, the rate of the code is just the inverse of the number of sets every vertex is contained in, that is,
\exp(-O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
.

Let us describe the decoder:

1.

We set
\delta_{0}=O(\sqrt[3]{\delta_{in}})
.

2.

We use the generating algorithm of the outer decoder, where we interpret the list indexes to the starting set
u
, as inputs to
C_{list-u}(u,i,\cdot)
.

3.

We call the outer decoder circuits where every time an inner list
g_{j}\in\mathcal{I}_{u^{\prime}}
is called, we simulate it via
C_{list-u}(u^{\prime},j,\cdot)
.

Fix
f\in{\cal B}_{1-\varepsilon,\delta_{in}}(w)
. By
5.9

f\in{\cal B}_{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},2\delta_{in}}(w_{In_{u}})
for all but
\frac{1}{2\log^{10}(N)}
of the
u\in U
. Thus by
Lemma

5.6
we can find a list
\mathcal{I}_{u}
of size
<\frac{10}{\varepsilon}
containing some function that is
\delta_{0}=O(\sqrt[3]{\delta_{in}})
-close to
f
. That is,
f\in{\cal B}_{\frac{1}{\log^{10}(N)},\delta_{0}}(\mathcal{I})
. Thus we can use the outer decoder in
Theorem

5.10
to recover a function that is
\delta_{out}=\exp(102\frac{1}{\varepsilon})\delta_{0}
-close to
f
(which is less than
0.01
by our parameter setting).

Finally for our chosen parameters:

1.

The routing scheme queries lists of
\log^{3}(N)\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
vertices.

2.

To prepare each list, the inner decoder is instantiated
O(\frac{\log\log(N)}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}})
times, and in each instantiation,
O(\frac{\log\log(N)}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}})
points are queried by
C_{list-u}
.

3.

Every query for the inner decoder requires
\poly(\frac{1}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\delta_{in}})
points from the original lists
\mathcal{L}
.

4.

Besides the list construction, the outer decoder tests distance between
O(\log^{3}(N)\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon})
pairs of functions, up to accuracy of
\geq\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}
. This requires at most
O(\log^{3}(N)\cdot\frac{1}{\varepsilon\delta_{in}})
points in total.

Thus the total query complexity is indeed
\log^{3}(N)\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))\poly(\log\log(N))
.

5.7
Circuit Implementation

To implement a circuit of the outer decoder we perform the following.

1.

We need call
C_{\mathcal{O}}^{T}(u,u^{\prime})
in order to route. For this we need to also call
\poly\log(N)\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))
calls to
C_{list-u}
, in order to implement the oracle for
T
. Fortunately we can make all calls in parallel by
5.5
. Therefore, this step is implementable in size
(\log N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
and depth
\tilde{O}(\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))\log^{2}\log N
.

2.

After that, we need to compute what is the index of a final
g_{m}
in the list of
u^{\prime}
that is close to the function
f
. As in the outer decoder for
Theorem

3.4
, we can do this with Savich’s algorithm in size
(\log N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
and depth
\tilde{O}(\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))\log^{2}\log N
. We omit the details since it is similar to those in
Lemma

3.5
.

All in all, the circuit implementation of the decoder for
Theorem

5.1
has size
(\log N)^{\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))}
and depth
\tilde{O}(\exp(O(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}))\log^{2}\log N
.

6
The Main Construction: KMS Codes

In this section we construct the set systems in
Proposition

4.3
and
Proposition

5.4
which arise from a form of high dimensional expanders called
local spectral expanders
and in particular a variant
[
58
]
of the elegant coset-complex HDX of Kaufman and Oppenheim
[
59
]
.

Roadmap

The section is ordered as follows. In
Section

6.1
we begin with some preliminaries on HDX. In
Section

6.2
we present a family of HDX known as Kac-Moody-Steinberg complexes (KMS complexes), that were constructed and analyzed in
[
58
]
, and in
Section

6.3
discuss their local structure. In
Section

6.4
, we use this structure to construct (subset internal) local routing schemes. In
Section

6.5
we define the set systems in
Proposition

4.3
and
Proposition

5.4
, and in
Section

6.6
prove
Proposition

4.3
and
Proposition

5.4
in full. We end with
Section

6.7
constructing circuits that implement neighborhood relations in the KMS complexes and bound the circuit complexity of the implementations of the set systems.

6.1
Preliminaries on High Dimensional Expanders

We will give only a brief primer on HDX necessary for our needs below. For a more comprehensive background we refer the reader e.g. to
[
93
]
and the references therein.

Simplicial Complexes

A
d
-dimensional simplicial complex

X
consists of a
(d+1)
-uniform hypergraph
X(d)
together with its downward closure

X=X(-1)\cup X(0)\cup\ldots\cup X(d),

where
X(i)\subseteq\binom{[N]}{i}
, called the ‘
i
-faces’ or ‘
(i+1)
-sets’, are all
(i+1)
-size subsets that sit in some hyperedge in
X(d)
, and
X(-1)=\{\emptyset\}
. Given a face
t\in X(i)
, the
link
of
t
is the sub-complex induced by localizing to faces that include
t
, that is
X_{t}\coloneqq\left\{{s\setminus t\in X}:{s\in X,s\supseteq t}\right\}
. We say
X
is
connected
if the base graph with vertices
X_{t}(0)
and edges
X_{t}(1)
of every link
X_{t}
is connected. The
inclusion graph

(X(k),X(i))
is the bipartite graph between
k
-faces and
i
-faces of
X
where edges are given by inclusion. The related
swap graph
(or
swap walk
) between
(X(k),X(i))
connects
k
-sets and
i
-sets of
X
such that
s\sim t
if the disjoint union
s\bigsqcup t\in X(k+i+1)
.
For
k<d
, The
k
-skeleton of a simplicial complex
X
is
X^{\leq k}=X(-1)\cup X(0)\cup\ldots\cup X(k)
.

Partite complexes

A
(d-1)
-dimensional complex is
d
-
partite
if its vertices can be partitioned into
d
‘parts’ such that each top-level face has one part from each component. We denote by
X[1],X[2],...,X[d]\subseteq X(0)
each part (we emphasize the square brackets denote the different parts of vertices). For an index set
C=\left\{{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{j}}\right\}\subseteq\left\{{1,2,\ldots,d}\right\}
we also denote by
X[C]\subseteq X(j-1)
all
(j-1)
-faces
\left\{{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{j}}\right\}\in X(j-1)
such that
v_{1}\in X[i_{1}]
,
v_{2}\in X[i_{2}]
,…,
v_{j}\in X[i_{j}]
(up to a change in their order). We call these
C
-colored faces. For a face
t
we denote by
color(t)
the index set
C
such that
t\in X[C]
. We also denote by
X^{C}=\bigcup_{C^{\prime}\subseteq C}X[C^{\prime}]
. We say that a partite complex is
regular
, if the number of top-level faces containing a set
t\in X
only depends on the color of
t
. We say that
X
is
fully regular
if
X
and all its links are regular.

Sometimes we also consider the colored-swap graph
(X[I],X[J])
which is a subgraph of the swap graph above, only containing vertices in
X[I]
and
X[J]
.

High dimensional expanders

Recall that a graph
G
is a
\lambda
-expander if the second largest eigenvalue of its normalized random walk operator is
\leq\lambda
. The normalized adjacency operator is the
V\times V
real-valued matrix

A(v,u)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{deg(v)}&\left\{{v,u}\right\}\in E\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

For simplicity we define high dimensional expanders only for fully regular partite complexes. We say that
X
is a
\lambda
-high dimensional expander if for every
t\in X^{\leq d-2}
and every
i,j\notin color(t)
, the bipartite graph between
X_{t}[i]
and
X_{t}[j]
is a
\lambda
-expander.
40
40

40

This is also the definition even if
X_{t}
is not fully regular, except that in this case we need put weights on the edges of this graph.

6.1.1
Theorems and Facts on HDX

Here we mention briefly some of the facts on HDX.

Expansion of inclusion and swap graphs

The inclusion and swap graphs are good expanders.

Theorem 6.1

(
[
24
]
)
.

Let
X
be a
d
-dimensional
\frac{1}{2d}
-one sided HDX. Then the inclusion graph
(X(k),X(i))
is an
O(\sqrt{\frac{i}{k}})
-one sided expander.

We note that in general one does not expect to do better than this bound as sets of the form
\left\{{s\in X(k)}\right\}{v_{0}\in s}\subseteq X(k)
do not expand in the inclusion graph more than
\approx\sqrt{\frac{i}{k}}
, but the swap graphs do expand better
[
57
,
97
,
98
,
99
]
:

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
X
be a
d
-partite
\lambda
-one sided HDX. Then for every
I,J\subseteq\left\{{1,2,\ldots,d}\right\}
of sizes
i,k
, the swap graph
(X[I],X[J])
is a
(i+k)\lambda
-one sided expander.

6.2
Coset and KMS Complexes

In this subsection, we give an instantiation of the KMS complexes described in
[
58
]
, which are a variant of the complexes in
[
59
]
.

KMS complexes are coset complexes. That is, they are parametrized by some finite group
G
and subgroups
K_{1},K_{2},\dots,K_{d}
. The vertices of the coset complex
X=X(G;K_{1},K_{2},\dots,K_{d})
are cosets

X(0)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}gK_{i}

and the top level sets are

X(d-1)=\left\{{\left\{{gK_{1},gK_{2},\dots,gK_{d}}\right\}}:{g\in G}\right\}.

Equivalently these are all sets
\left\{{g_{1}K_{1},g_{2}K_{2},\dots,g_{d}K_{d}}\right\}
such that
\bigcap_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}K_{i}\neq\emptyset
. One can verify that the intermediate level sets
X(i)
have a similar description within each color set. That is for
C=\left\{{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{j}}\right\}
,
X[C]
is the set of
\bigcap_{i\in C}g_{i}K_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

It is a classical fact that the mapping

\left\{{g_{1}K_{i_{1}},g_{2}K_{i_{2}},\dots,g_{r}K_{i_{r}}}\right\}\in X[C]\longmapsto\bigcap_{i\in C}g_{i}K_{i}

is a bijection from faces to cosets of
K_{C}\coloneqq\bigcap_{i\in C}K_{i}
. Moreover, faces
s,t\in X
satisfy
s\subseteq t
if and only if
t\cong gK_{C_{1}},s\cong g^{\prime}K_{C_{2}}
such that
C_{1}\subseteq C_{2}
and
gK_{C_{1}}\supseteq g^{\prime}K_{C_{2}}
.

The complexes we use are based on the group
G=SL_{d}(R)
,
d\times d
matrices with determinant
1
, where
R=\nicefrac{{\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}[t]}}{{\phi(t)}}\cong\mathbb{F}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\kappa}}
, i.e.
\phi(t)
is a degree
\kappa
irreducible polynomial.

Our subgroups
K_{i}
are generated by elementary unipotent matrices.

Definition 6.3

(Elementary Unipotent Matrices)
.

For
i,j\in\left\{{1,2,\dots,d}\right\}
with
i\neq j
and
r\in R
, let
e_{i,j}(r)
denote the unipotent elementary matrix in
G
such that
r
appears in the
(i,j)
-entry, and all other off-diagonal entries are zero, i.e., the identity matrix with the
(i,j)
th entry replaced by
r
.

Definition 6.4

(Subgroups
K_{i}
)
.

For each
i\in\left\{{1,2,\dots,d-1}\right\}
, let
K_{i}
be

K_{i}=\langle\left\{{e_{j,j+1}(a):j\in[d]\setminus\left\{{i}\right\},\,a\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}}\right\}\cup\left\{{e_{d,1}(at):a\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}}\right\}\rangle.

In addition, let

K_{d}=\langle\left\{{e_{j,j+1}(a):j\in[d-1],a\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}}\right\}\rangle.

It is observed (see e.g.
[
58
]
) that all these groups are isomorphic, and that
K_{d}
are just upper triangular matrices with
1
’s on the diagonal, and elements in the field
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}
above the diagonal (the variable
t
doesn’t appear). In particular, every
|K_{i}|={\mathfrak{q}}^{\binom{d}{2}}
and therefore
|X(0)|=d{\mathfrak{q}}^{-\binom{d}{2}}|SL_{d}(R)|={\mathfrak{q}}^{\Omega(d^{2}\kappa)}
.
In,
[
100
, Theorem 3.6]
, the authors prove these complexes are connected and strongly explicit. Unfortunately, the coset bit representation for which they show strong explicitness has substantial slack (i.e. the bit-length is much larger than
\log|X(0)|
), so cannot be used in our context (c.f. the discussion in
Section

3.3.4
). In
Section

6.7
, we show how to implement these complexes as small, low depth circuits with negligible slack.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}
be a prime power and
d,\kappa>0
be integers. Define the complex
\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{X}^{{\mathfrak{q}},d,\kappa}
to be the coset complex defined using the group and subgroups above.

6.3
Links of
\mathfrak{X}

As alluded to, these complexes are high dimensional expanders.
41
41

41

Technically due to slight differences in the notion of local spectral expansion, this lemma uses some standard machinery from
[
57
]
to translate between the two, but we omit the details. An explicit translation is given in
[
101
]
.

Lemma 6.6

(
[
100
]
,
[
58
]
)
.

The complex
\mathfrak{X}
is a
\frac{10}{\sqrt{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
-high dimensional expander. Moreover, every vertex is connected to at most
\leq{\mathfrak{q}}^{\Theta(d^{2})}
top level faces, independent of
\kappa
.

We can give a very explicit construction of the links using the following complex called ‘the opposition complex’.
42
42

42

In fact,
[
102
]
defines opposition complexes to be a more general type of complex, but we will use the name opposition complex to denote only the complex we describe.

Fix
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{d}
, and let
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{d}
be the standard basis. For
j=1,2,\dots,d-1
, let
E_{j}=span\left\{{e_{1},...,e_{j}}\right\}
. The opposition complex
P
is a
(d-1)
-partite complex whose vertices are

P[i]=\left\{{W\subseteq\mathcal{V}}:{W\text{ is a linear subspace, }\dim(W)=i,\text{ and }W+E_{d-i}=\mathcal{V}}\right\}.

Edges are pairs
\left\{{W,W^{\prime}}\right\}
such that (up to reordering)
W\subset W^{\prime}
,
i
-faces are nested sequence of
i
subspaces:
\{W_{1},W_{2},\ldots,W_{i}\}
such that
W_{1}\subset W_{2}...
up to re-ordering, and top level faces are given by complete flags (again up to re-ordering):

P(d-1)=\left\{{\left\{{W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},\dots,W_{d-1}}\right\}}:{W_{1}\subset W_{2}\subset\dots\subset W_{d-1}}\right\}.

Claim 6.7

(
[
102
]
, see discussion in
[
58
,
103
]
)
.

For every
gK_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}(0)
, the link
\mathfrak{X}_{gK_{i}}
is isomorphic to the opposition complex
P
. Moreover, the part
\mathfrak{X}_{gK_{i}}[j]
is sent to
P[j-i]
under this isomorphism (modulo
d-1
).

We also note that the opposition complex can be ‘flipped’ upside down, that is,

Claim 6.8
.

There is an automorphism of
P
that sends
P[i]
to
P[d-i]
and vice versa.

Proof.

First note that the choice of the standard basis in the definition of
P
is arbitrary, and we can in fact use any ordered basis and get an isomorphic complex. We will describe an automorphism
\phi
of
P
that sends subspaces of
\mathcal{V}
to subspaces of
\mathcal{V}
. The automorphism will map
W\in P[i]
to
\phi(W)\in P[d-i]
, and for every
W\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
W+E_{d-i}=V
iff
\phi(W)+\tilde{E}_{i}=\mathcal{V}
, for appropriately defined
\tilde{E}_{i}
.

Let us describe the automorphism first. For a vector
v=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{d})\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}^{d}
let
v^{\perp}
be the linear functional
v^{\perp}(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{d})=v_{1}u_{1}+v_{2}u_{2}+\dots+v_{d}u_{d}
. For any subspace
W\subseteq V
, let
\phi(W)=\left\{{v\in\mathcal{V}}:{\forall w\in W,w^{\perp}(v)=0}\right\}
. Note that
\dim(\phi(W))=d-dim(W)
(fixing a basis
w_{1},...w_{i}
to
W
, the equations
w_{j}^{\perp}(v)=0
are independent linear equations. The nullspace of these equations is precisely
\phi(W)
).
Now let us take
f_{1}=e_{d},f_{2}=e_{d-1},\dots,f_{d-1}=e_{2},f_{d}=e_{1}
and set
\tilde{E}_{i}=span(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{i})
. One can verify that
\phi(E_{i})=\tilde{E}_{d-i}
. We claim that
W+E_{d-i}=\mathcal{V}
if and only if
\phi(W)+\tilde{E}_{i}=\mathcal{V}
. Indeed, this is equivalent to proving that
W+E_{d-i}=\mathcal{V}
if and only if
\phi(W)\cap\tilde{E}_{i}=\left\{{0}\right\}
(since
\dim(\phi(W)+\tilde{E}_{i})=d-\dim(\phi(W)\cap\tilde{E}_{i})
). Indeed,
W+E_{d-i}=V
if and only if
\phi(W+E_{i})=\left\{{0}\right\}
. By the fact that
W\cup E_{i}
spans
W+E_{i}
,
\phi(W+E_{i})=\phi(W)\cap\phi(E_{i})=\phi(W)+\tilde{E}_{i}
so the claim is proven.
∎

The following corollary is direct from the last claim so we omit its proof.

Corollary 6.9
.

For every
gK_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}(0)
, the link
\mathfrak{X}_{gK_{i}}
is isomorphic to the opposition complex
P
. Moreover, the part
\mathfrak{X}_{gK_{i}}[j]
is sent to
P[d-j+i]
under this isomorphism (modulo
d-1
).

Finally, we get the following general symmetry guarantee.

Claim 6.10
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\subseteq[d]
be two sets of colors such that there is an isomorphism of the cycle
\phi:[d]\to[d]
that maps
\phi(C_{1})=C_{2}
(that is,
\phi(x)=\pm x+c
modulo
d
, for some
c\in[d]
). Then for every
x\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]
and
y\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
there is an isomorphism such that for every color
C\subseteq[d]
,
\mathfrak{X}_{x}[C]
is mapped to
\mathfrak{X}_{y}[\phi(C)]
.

Proof.

If
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are singletons then this just follows from
6.7
and
Corollary

6.9
. Otherwise write
C_{1}=\left\{{i}\right\}\cup C_{1}^{\prime}
and
C_{2}=\left\{{i^{\prime}}\right\}\cup C_{2}^{\prime}
where
i^{\prime}=\phi(i)
. Let
x=\left\{{x_{1}}\right\}\cup x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]
such that
x\in\mathfrak{X}[i]
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}^{\prime}]
. Let
y_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}[i^{\prime}]
and by the claim for singletons there is an isomorphism between
\mathfrak{X}_{x_{1}}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{y_{1}}
that sends
C_{1}^{\prime}
to
\phi(C_{1}^{\prime})=C_{2}^{\prime}
. In particular, the set
x^{\prime}\mapsto y^{\prime}
for some
y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}^{\prime}]
. Moreover, this is an isomorphism of complexes, so in particular the link
\mathfrak{X}_{x}=(\mathfrak{X}_{x_{1}})_{x^{\prime}}\cong(\mathfrak{X}_{y_{1}})_{y^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{X}_{y}
by this same isomorphism. The claim follows.
∎

6.4
Routing and the KMS Complexes

In this subsection we establish a routing scheme for this complex that will in turn give rise to the routing schemes in
Proposition

4.3
and
Proposition

5.4
. The idea is as follows. We will first find a deterministic routing scheme for swap walk graphs between two disjoint colors
\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]
and
\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
. Then we will use the fact that the group
SL_{d}(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}})
acts on the complex and that the action is edge-transitive for edges between
\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]
and
\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
. We will use the group to randomize the routing scheme sufficiently so that it will have low congestion.

In the last step we will also present a subset internal routing scheme on
\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}],\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
that is given an oracle for membership on some large
T\subseteq\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]\cup\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
, and produces a path between most
s_{1},s_{2}
such that all intermediate vertices in the path are in
T
. This scheme will also have low congestion, and is crucial for
Theorem

5.1
.

6.4.1
A Deterministic Routing Scheme

We start by designing a deterministic routing algorithm between any two
\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}],\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
such that the colors
C_{1},C_{2}
are disjoint.

Recall for every color
C\subseteq[d]
, the faces of
\mathfrak{X}[C]
can be identified with cosets of
K_{C}=\bigcap_{i\in C}K_{i}
since every
s\in\mathfrak{X}[C]
is equal to
s=\left\{{gK_{i}}\right\}_{i\in C}
for some
g\in G
, and we can identify it with
gK_{C}
which is the common intersection of all vertices in
s
(the formal proof follows from the definitions and is therefore omitted). As
s_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}],s_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
are connected if and only if
s_{1}\cup s_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}\cup C_{2}]
, we can also identify edges in the swap walk graph with
gK_{C_{1}\cup C_{2}}
.

Claim 6.11

(Neighborhood Structure)
.

Fix
C_{1},C_{2}\subseteq[d]
disjoint. The
C_{2}
-neighborhood of a coset
gK_{C_{1}}\in\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]
is the set:

\left\{{ghK_{C_{2}}:h\in K_{C_{1}}}\right\}.

Remark 6.12
.

Given this claim, the problem of finding a path of length
\leq t
from (say)
AK_{C_{1}}
to
BK_{C_{2}}
reduces to finding a decomposition of
A^{-1}B=h_{1}h_{2}\dots h_{t-1}
such that
h_{2k+1}\in K_{C_{1}}
and
h_{2k}\in K_{C_{2}}
(of course, some can be the identity), because we can take the path of the form

AK_{C_{1}},Ah_{1}K_{C_{2}},Ah_{1}h_{2}K_{C_{1}},\dots,Ah_{1}h_{2}\dots h_{t-1}K_{C_{x}}=BK_{C_{x}}

and then, if
K_{C_{x}}=K_{C_{2}}
we are done, and otherwise we just take the final step from
BK_{C_{1}}
to
BK_{C_{2}}
. Let us prove the claim for completeness.

Proof of
6.11
.

The neighbors of
gK_{C_{1}}
are all
g^{\prime}K_{C_{2}}
such that
gK_{C_{1}}\cap g^{\prime}K_{C_{2}}\neq\emptyset
. Clearly if
g^{\prime}=gh
for
h\in K_{C_{1}}
then the intersection is not empty. Conversely, if the intersection contains some element
g^{\prime\prime}
then by definition of
gK_{C_{1}}
,
g^{\prime\prime}=gh
for some
h\in K_{C_{1}}
, and therefore
g^{\prime}K_{C_{2}}=g^{\prime\prime}K_{C_{2}}=ghK_{C_{2}}
.
∎

In our graphs, we will always be able to take short steps that correspond to the elementary matrices that generate the subgroups.

Corollary 6.13
.

Let
e_{j,j+1}(r)
be one of the generators in the definition of the subgroups
K_{1},K_{2},\dots,K_{d}
(i.e.
r\in\mathbb{F}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
if
j\neq d
and
r=r^{\prime}t
for
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
if
j=d
). Then for any disjoint
C_{1},C_{2}\subseteq[d]
, and any coset
gK_{C_{1}}
, there is a path of length
\leq 2
from
gK_{C_{1}}
to
ge_{j,j+1}(r)K_{C_{1}}
.

Proof.

Recall that the colors tell us which generators
are missing
from the subgroups. Observe that
C_{1}\cap C_{2}=\emptyset
and therefore
j
cannot be in both color sets, which means that the matrices of the form
e_{j,j+1}(r)
must be in one of the subgroups
K_{C_{1}},K_{C_{2}}
. If
j\notin C_{1}
then
e_{j,j+1}(r)\in K_{C_{1}}
and then
gK_{C_{1}}=ge_{j,j+1}(r)K_{C_{1}}
so techincally there is a path of length
0
from the coset to itself.
Otherwise
j\notin K_{C_{2}}
and we can walk
(gK_{C_{1}},gK_{C_{2}},ge_{j,j+1}(r)K_{C_{1}})
since
e_{j,j+1}(r)\in K_{C_{2}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.14

(Routing Scheme)
.

Let
C_{1},C_{2}\subseteq[d]
be two disjoint sets of colors. Then the swap walk graph between
\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]=\left\{{gK_{C_{1}}}\right\}_{g\in G}
and
\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]=\left\{{gK_{C_{2}}}\right\}_{g\in G}
admits a deterministic routing scheme
\mathfrak{X}
. The maximal length of a path is
O(\poly(d)\kappa^{\log_{2}5})
.

Proof.

To construct a path between every pair of vertices it is enough to give a path from the coset (vertex)
K_{C_{1}}
to every vertex in
\mathfrak{X}[C_{1}]
or
\mathfrak{X}[C_{2}]
. To go from a generic
AK_{C_{i}}
to
BK_{C_{j}}
we may then just concatenate their paths through
K_{C_{1}}
.
Also, note that if we have a path of length
t
from
K_{C_{1}}
to
AK_{C_{1}}
then we also have a path of length
t+1
to
AK_{C_{2}}
since
AK_{C_{1}}\sim AK_{C_{2}}
. Therefore, it suffices to construct a path from
K_{C_{1}}
to
AK_{C_{1}}
for any
A\in SL_{d}(R)
. From now on let us fix
A\in SL_{d}(R)
.

Now by
Remark

6.12
and
Corollary

6.13
, it suffices to decompose
A
to
O(\poly(d)\kappa^{5})
generators, i.e. as a product of
O(\poly(d)\kappa^{5})
elementary matrices
e_{j,j+1}(r)
as in the subgroup definitions.

It is known that every
A\in SL_{d}(R)
is a product of
O(d^{2})
elementary unipotent matrices
43
43

43

Indeed this is just by Gaussian elimination.

e_{i,j}(r)
and thus it suffices to find such a decomposition assuming
A=e_{i,j}(r)
for some
i,j
and
r\in R
. In fact, we claim it is even enough to prove this just for
r
of the form
r=t\cdot r^{\prime}(t)
, that is, for polynomials with no constant part. The reason is the following. Suppose we know how to write
e_{i,j}(r)
for every polynomial of the form
r=tr^{\prime}(t)
. Then because we are working over
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}[t]/(\phi(t))
and not
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}[t]
, this means that
\phi(t)\equiv 0
in our ring. As this polynomial is irreducible, this means that
\phi(0)\neq 0
, and in particular, that it has a non-zero constant part. Thus if
\phi(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\kappa}\alpha_{j}t^{j}
, then

1=-t(\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{0}}t^{j-1}).

This means that by writing
e_{i,j}(t(\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{0}}t^{j-1}))
we are effectively writing a constant. Of course, for any other polynomial with a constant part
q(t)=a+tq^{\prime}(t)
we can just write the polynomial

t\cdot a(\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\alpha_{0}}t^{j-1})+tq^{\prime}(t)

which is equivalent to it. Thus if we can write any polynomial of the form
tr^{\prime}(t)
, we can also write all polynomials in
R
.
44
44

44

This seems unnecessarily complicated since we actually have
e_{i,j}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{q}}
constant whenever
i<j\neq d
. However, to get to the matrices
e_{i,d}(\alpha)
using these generators we actually need to go to a polynomial of large degree.

We now proceed by induction on
\deg(r^{\prime})
. Let
T(m)
be the maximal number of elements from
K_{C_{1}}\cup K_{C_{2}}
required to write
tr^{\prime}
such that
\deg(r^{\prime})\leq m
.

Recall the following formulas that can be easily verified:

1.

For any
i,j
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in R

e_{i,j}(r_{1})e_{i,j}(r_{2})=e_{i,j}(r_{1}+r_{2})

(6)

2.

For any
3
distinct
i,j,\ell
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in R

[e_{i,\ell}(r_{1}),e_{\ell,j}(r_{2})]=e_{i,j}(r_{1}\cdot r_{2})

(7)

where the brackets are the commutator
[g,h]=ghg^{-1}h^{-1}

Let us do the induction step proving that
T(2^{m})\leq 5T(2^{m-1})
. Then, below, we prove that
T(1)=O(d)
by some tedious computations. Solving the recursion we get that
T(\kappa)\leq O(d\kappa^{\log_{2}5})
which proves the assertion. Let
r^{\prime}
be a polynomial of degree
